TALKS ON HEALTH.

EBY & F.5.N.111.1 DOCTOR. 3

WHAT A TONIC IS FOR.

If you are taking a tonic, remember that the tonic in not an end in itself, but only the means to an end. The real object is to encourage you to take good food: if the tonic does not make you eat, it is useless Your body eau no more work without fool than an engine can go without coal in the furnace and water in the boiler. Try the experiment of leaving out the coal and the water, and placing a bottle of tonic in the boiler, and you have a picture of the state of affairs in your own body when you solemnly drink your "Muff" and decline your food. Or give your tonic to your pony, and see if he will pull the cart without a good feed of corn. A tonic is an aid to appetite, not a eubatitute for good food.

BOOTS AND BUNIONS.

am not going to dismiss the cure ef bunions until you hair isaight yourself a properly-shaped pair of hoot, 1,0,0 lace, uf mankind who have not yet learnt the blessings of civilisation and walk barefoot, have no bunions. I have seen native women in Africa carrying 'pitchers of water on their heads. walking with the most stately grooe . - and putting to shame the ugly gad of the civilised " woman, with her pinching boots, bunioned feet, and hobble skirt. Also 1 complain of you because you came to in, with a bunion of t wenty yearn standing and ask me to mire it. You might as well ask me to put straight the crooked branch of nip old oak tree. 'l he harm is done. Au operation can be performed for very large bunions, lint you never consent to have it done. Bunions must die out with the people who have them you must pltmse see to it that none of your children have deformed feet.

SKIN STRUCTURES. When a piece of skin is examined under a pwwerful microscope, many at: lactates may -he seen. First of all, there are the stake which wear off and are roplared by others which grow up from the deeper layers of the skin. 'then we can see the little sweat-glands; they have a ceded duct like • A cork-screw, and the sweat pours out of each little gland along this duct. In some cases it can be KePA that the opening of the duct is blocked up bv a collection of the scales and also by dirt. Again, we find other glands, called sebaceous glands, which secrete an oily substance to keep the skin healthy and supple. Especially are these glands found near hairs, and they supply the natural oil to preserve the hair. Some- times the action of these glands is deficient, and then the hair is dry and cracks, and some simple grease tas to be applied to supply the deficiency.

THE IMPORTANCE OF BATHING. We are now in a position to understand the importance of bathing the skin. The dead scales of the swent-glands and the grease-glands must be kept clear so that the prorincts of the glands can r,cape freely : deleterious matter is expelled from the body by the activity of the skin, and it is liarmiul tc impede its free action. The skin is not 3 dead covering, but alive and working and growing ; the skin is just as much an organ as the kiiney

WHY ALCOHOL COOLS THE BLOOD. Then in the skin there are blood vessels and nerves. The vessels are not rigid like pipe-stems, but ran alter their calibre making themselves large or small according to the temperature of the body. On a hot summer's day the vessels are swollen, and you look as red as a tomato; on a cold day, with a keen east wind blowing, the vessels are shrunk to a small calibre to prev-mt the blood being cooled by exposure to the air. The blood, on a cold day. is kept warm by being retained inside the body', when yon have just completed making your century in the cricket match. all the Mood is sent to the surface to get cooled down. Remember that to get cool the blood is seat to the Anto- ine, ; to get hot the blood is wilt to the inner parts and kept away from the sit,- Inc, Now you understand why alcohol zco:s he blood. because it causes a flushing of the skin and allows the heat to escape. Never take alcohol "to keep th,• cold out' - it lets the cold in. Finally, in the skin there are sensitive nerve, ; that is why a plunge in a cold bath is no refreshing- it stimulates the thousands of sensitive nerves that are in the skin, and this stimulation reacts on the whole nervous system. Thus there is much to be learnt from the study of the akin.

A JUNIOR TORY ON THE TORY SPLIT

The spectacle of the Tory party rent by grave differences on policy is not at all to the liking of the Editor of the" Lancashire and Cheshire Junior Unionist S' who has a few unpalatable things to say in the current issue of that publication. While striving to make out that there is no plot to depose Mr. Balfour, the writer of the article on " Hedge:s and Ditchers " uses language which plainly indicates his concern at the revolt against the present leadership of the party. The Halsbury banquet, he says, provides evidence that disintegrating forces are at work which are loosening discipline and disorganizing the party machine, tie continues : Expressions of goodsill are very well, but nothing rein alter the plain facts that at a very critical moment, when the chiefs of the Unionist Party were btrenuouely advocating what they believed to be the only safe policy, a strong section of their followers thought it their duty to come into active antagonism against them. This may not indicaeo aby desire to make a demonstration against Mr. Balfour's leadership, but it certainly indicates that tbe Unionist Party is badly cut of hand. Right br wrong, a leader is a leader, and. so long ache remains leader, the whole machinery and organisation requires, not merely proleseions of loyalty, but faith in his guidance. Formal refusal to areeept such guid- ance renders !eldership humiliation. If a leader no longer obtains the confidence of his party it is time that he went. So long, however, as any man remains in command, it is obvious that public rejection of his advice and repudiation of his tactics can only create indiecipline and tench r his position untenable. It would be difficult ect imagine aby situation more demoralising than that which has been created by this eo-callcd " difference a opinion upon a course of pro- cedure." The spectacle t t Lord Lansdowne pulling in cne direction and Lord Ilalsbury in the other is bad enough for most of an, Lot when we reach a point at which Mr. Balfour announces hie intention of standing or falling with Lord Lansdowne because the latter's policy is the only one consistent with common sense, and Mr. Austen Chamberlain, whew Mr. Balfour has constituted his principal lieutenant in the !louse of Commons, immediately replies that the course advocated by Mr. Palfour means " disgrace" and " disaster." surely the rank and file may be t soused if they ask what on earth it all means.

THE IMPORTANCE OF LAND VALUATION.

By E. G Hemmerde, K.C.

It is commonly said that when a Government eters begins to recede in popular favour it can never recover its lost prsition. We have, however, in the history of the present Government a remarkable exception to this rule. I atti ibute the astotorling recovery of the Govern- ment from the eet backs experienced prior to 19b9 aluvost entirely to the Land Clauses of the Budget. The art of political warfare lies in aggression. From 1900 to 1909 the Liberal party was alwaps on the defeneive. From the date of the it troduction of the Budget of 1960 the Liberal party has been attacking, the Tory party bas been on the defensive, and rot only on the defensive, but defend- ing a position which its wisest generals know t be incapiNe ef defence. In 19U: ewers of Tory candidates in the north of England and Scotland pledged themselves, and were instigated to do so by their own headqueiters, to the rating of Land Values. Without a complete and universal valuation of site values the rating v f Land Values is impossible- It is therefore not sur- prising that a potty, many if whose ablest member. believe in the principle of the rating or taxation of Land Velure, should view the opposition to the first step by which alone such rating or taxation of Land V i alues s rendered possible, with perplexity amount- kg to dismay. Whit is the mapic in Land Valuation which has put new life into Liberalism and has confounded all its tnen.ies? Before 1909 not only was Toryism pining strength, but Socialism also. Now both give way to the new and enlightened phase of Liberalivm. Why is it? Simply because in Lard Valuation men are begin- Bing to see the key which will unlock a vavt national treasure house. and the solution of great and threaten- ing social problems. The greatest evils in fur catiooal life to-day are— (l) the depopulation of our rival district,; (.2) the terrible conditions in which millions of people live; (3) the haunting dread of unemplojmeot. For all of these the valuation of land and the subsequent use of this valuatit n, a ill provide a partial and as many of us think almost a total remedy.

Our rural districts are depopulated not because the attractions of the town magnetuse the young ambitious people, but because the attracione of the country are deliberately denied to them. If we are to keep a ham y and contented peasantry we roust give them a ready access to the soil. Tceday not only is it extremely diflicu% for the rural labourers to get access to the soil, tut thousands and thousands of young people are driven out of the country districts into the towns for lack of housing accommodation for the young people who snot to marry. Land mow p ly beats down the wages of the agricultural labourers, and the studied insolence of the parasitic Press then proceeds to explain .the lack of housing accommcda- non in the rural districts by the fact that the rural labourer cannot afford to pay a fair rent for a docent cott4 e. If the rural labourer was properly paid he could afford to pay for a decent cottage. The only way to secure Lim a proper wage is by the economic pressure of Land Values Taxation. HOUSE FURNISHING.

===== 0002601_19110812_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
By Philip Snowden, M P SHOULD M.P.'S BE PAID ?

The House of Commons is discussing this week the oft-debated question of the Pay- ment of Members of Parliament. It is a fairly well-established fact that hundreds of years ago Members of the English Parlia- ment were paid, and that the law under which this was done has never been repealed. But as the amount which was considered sufficient to meet the expenses of a member of the House of Commons in those days was equal to about £l4 a year it would hardly be worth the while of any present member of Parliament to claim his legal rights under that statute. A LONG AGITATION.

But we need not go so far back to ascertain that the idea of paying members of Parlia- ment in this country is one which has been long held as an article in the democratic creed. In 1780, when Major Cartwright and Horne Tooke were leading a great democratic agitation in England, this question was very much to the front. In that year the Whigs cuivened a great Convention to draw up a Reform Charter. This Convention was pre- sided over by the young Whig orator, Charles James Fox, and the demand for Payment of Members was inserted in the programme. Sixty years later it was revived in the more famous Chartist programme, and forty years after that the "recognition of the principle of payment of members" was inserted in the Newcastle programme. More than 130 years after the Whigs first made the demand, the House of Commons, on the ' motion of the Government of the day, has taken up this question. In the meantime every other country in the world which has a Parliament has adopted to some extent the practice of paying its members. We would not advance that fact as alone justifying tha British House of Commons in following these examples, although it must carry some weight in the consideration of the question. But there seems from the point of Labour and Democracy an unanswerable case for the payment of members of the British Parlia- ment. If we accept the principle of democracy in politics then we are corn - pelted to concede that payment of members is a necessary part of the political machinery. In these days no one would openly say that Parliament should be confined to rich men who have the means to pay heavy election expenses and to support themselves there. If we concede the right of poor men to vote, then we must logically go further than this and admit that poor men should be able to select men to represent them, unhampered by the handicap of poverty.

===== 0002601_19110812_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO WAYS OF DOING IT.

There are two ways in which poor men may get into Parliament and be supported there. The one is the method which the trade unions have adopted, namely of payhig , the election expenses of members of their unions and of supporting them out of the union funds if they are returned. Even if that were a very desirable way of getting workingclass representation, it has now been made illegal by the decision of the House of Lords in a recent judgment. But I do not think that any supporter of the Labour Party has ever defended the system of trade union representation as being the most Admirable form of democratic representation. At the best it has been something of a makeshift. I do not think it is possible to defend the constitution of the Labour Party on the grounds of abstract logic. But it can be defended as a practical and useful way of getting working class representation. But the chief objection to a trade union Parliamentary Labour Party is the injustice it is to the trade unions them- selves. When a trade union candidate is suuessful he becomes not merely the repre- sentative, of the union which supports him. but the member for the constituency, and he looks after the interests not only of the trade unionists but of the whole body of workers in particular and of the whole country in general. It is not fair in such circumstances that the trade union should have to pay the election expenses and the cost of supporting the member in Parliament. ANOTHER OBJECTION.

There is another objection also to the merely trade union candidature. It narrows the field of selection. The candidate who has the financial backing of a trade union stands a far better chance of adoption than the man who must rely for the election expenses on the efforts to be made in the constituency. Trade unions should and will be represented in Parliament, because they have the organ- isation, but it has never been urged that I trade unionism alone should be the basis of a democratic party and the universal qualification for a democratic member of Parliament. The present Labour Party recognised that from the beginning. When they decided to go in for Parliamen- tary representation they invited the co-operation of other democratic organisa- tions such as the Socialists and the co-opera- tors. The whole body of unorganised labour might join the Labour party under its con- stitution. The justification for paying members of Parliament is in the hard fact that without it we cannot give the constituencies the choice of candidates they are entitled to have if democracy is to be a reality and not a sham. It is as humiliating for a constitu- ency to have to take its candidate from a voluntary society which pays the expenses as it is to have to take a rich man who pays his own expenses. The candidate should be in such a position of freedom that if he is rsturned he will be the servants of the constituency, or of a party which represents the, majority of the electo- ts, and net of at y section whose interests he must consider first becauselhe is paid by them. Payment of members by the State canjalone secure that. When a member is paid from public funds he becomes the servant of the public, and he will be under no obligation to put set time' interests before the general good. hs work of Parliament is becoming so x icting that men are now required to devote p •actically all their time to it. If members a-e last to be paid;from the State, and if trade unions are not to be allowe Ito promote

candidatures, then there is an end to Labour or working class representation in Parlia- ment. That would be a disaster to the State, for a House of Commons which was composed of men drawn from one class only, and which was barred to poor men would have no real authority to represent the people. That way revolution would lie. RAISING THE STANDARD.

There is little doubt in my opinion that the payment of mombers would tend to greatly raise the quality of Parliamentary candi- dates. The fear of the critic that it would lead to the encouragement of the professional politician seems to me to be the weakest of all the objections to the proposal. Why should we not have the professional poli- tician? Why should it be necessary and desirable to have the professional lawyer,the professions! doctor, the university pro- fessor, and not the professional politician? Politics require as much knowledge as any of the professions named, and why should not a man be encouraged to devote himself to the study of politics as a profession just the same as he would study to become a lawyer or a minister of religion? The objection about the professional politician seems to be based on the idea that it is dis- honorable to serve one's country for pay as a law-maker, but not as a soldier or a civil servant. If politics became a profession we should get better politicians. Now we have practically nothing but amateurs at the work, who take it up ass sort of finish to a successful business career, just as a man takes to growing orchids or collecting china in his old age. Surely the constituencies can be trusted to select the right kind of men. They are far more likely to do that when they have a wider choice. When they pay their members they will select them for their ability and not for their wealth. Of course we cannot expect constituencies which have always regarded the member of Parliament as a sort of universal provider or general relieving officer to take the higher point of view all at once. But very soon the change will come. A very drastic new Corrupt Practices Act will be needed to stop rich men from attempt- ing to bribe constituencies by offering to give the payment in subscriptions to local charities. But Payment of Members will be a very useful reform.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

Successful Re-opening Week

The re.openiug a the Royal Court Theatre on Saturday evening was welcomed by • large and moat appreciative audience and, notwithstanding the delight- ful summer weather of the past week, good houses have continued to be the rule. The elaborate scheme of re-decoration carried out during the holidays has celled forth general prsise and admiration. In comfort and attrartivenees, the royal Court now ranks high among provincial theatres. Great taste and artistic skill have been shown ia the decorative work, while tbe hope ,ved seating arrangement t in the stalls, pit and circle, cannot fail to commend themselves to putout,. The stage aAraction fir the re-opening week has taken the form of • Venetian drama in four acts entitled " The Tyrant," by the late Fred C. Secure- held. Founded on one of Victor Hugo'. work,. it grips the eedience by the briskness elite action and the intensity of the many thrilling situations which find a place in the story. Mr. Bulmer'e company gives an earnest and convincing performance, end each evening there have been spontaneous demonstrations of great cordiality in appreciation of the el 'rts of the principals First and foremost comes Miss Marie Dagmar, a favourite with Warrington audiences, who displays talent do, high order in the difficult role of Tittle,. See realizes Ii perfection the mazy contrasting emotions of the part, and is in turn flippant sod playful, highly dramatic and pathetic, u the prlgress of the plot warrants. Mr. A. Edward Brooke presents a cl,ver study of Homodel, the evenger. infusing remarkable realism and power into his wort. He is a mysterious and aweseme figure, ever haunted with the consuoring desire for vengeance and and it is his cunningly theught-out scheme which dominates the whole story. Mr. Bert Oates iinpereenates Angelo, the cruel and scrupulous tyrant, in such whole-hearted fashion es to earn the hostility of the " gods"— a testimony to the effectiveness of his acting. Miss Maud Steeple is well placed as Lady Katherine, wife of Angelo, one feature which com- mends her portrayal being its naturalness. Miss Holly Haelewood has a melenchrly purl Flomoders sister, but she succeeds in making it interesting. Some excellent comic by-play is pro- vided by Mr. Harold Holloway (Victor), Mr. Fred Luard 'Nobbo end Miss Nancy Nevrell Reginella. The scenery and effects are admirable. A choice selection of light music is provided by the orchestra under Mr. T. Beothroyd's conductorship.

IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

TiII: ABORTIVE Vo"TE oF CENTRE,

Ile would be • poor sort of Irishman indeed whine mind was not big with joy and hope when last Tuesday's newspaper brought Icm the tiding, of the historic debate of Monday last in the If use of Commons. For our part we say frankly that few ewes in Britain's Parliament during modern times have been quite so full of deep and potent significance to Ireland am was that enacted ralien the solid majority ! hurled bark Mr. Balfour's belated Tree of censure, on the divided force which has to persistently and so relentlessly fought our national ideal. To come to the debate itself, which later geLerations will probably esteem as one of the great landmarks in the political history of our time, Mr. Asquith did well at the beginning f his powerful speech t thank the Itickleo• leader of the Opposition for bringing forward Ids rote of censure. Nothing could have better served the pprpoies of the quiet strong man who as Prime Minister is to-day leading the progressive thought of the kingdom in an attack, n ov close to victory, on the privileges of the irreationsible cligarchy. The occaeion enabled the Government not only to state clearly and finally the unanswerable case for the use of the Royal' preronatire litat to secure an endorsement of that position from the united anti- Peersmajority in the If nose of Commons. With the main lines of the Governments case as stated in the Prime Ministers masterly opeer k, most politicians by this time must be tho °uglily familiar nor is there any reason to examine the logical sequence of the argil- meat on which Mr. Asquith based his canatitritional thesis. Mr. Ballotar—rately if ever heard to such poor effect and with ouch incoherence—with weak and nebulous idea of history vainly tried to show that the precedent of 1532 ail not epply. But, as Mr. Churchill later showed, that in every point of dal r- enee. lull presents a much stronger case for the use if the prerogative than 1832. There was but one election in IS3'2, nit three es Mr. Balfour in rather blundering fashion declared thie time there were two. the second being a conce,siou to Toryiern. Re eating all the worn-out allegations, the Leader of the Opposi- tion laid the whole emphasis of his apemb on Home Rule. And we Nationalists rejoice that whilst our own leader, Mr. Re.lruond, looks on keenly observant of the fight in its every phase the overdue just: ce of our cause finds no thorough • champion as Mr. Winston Churchill. After Mr. Reilmonire dignitled rebuke to the unmannerly con- duct of Mr. F. E. Smith, in which rebuke Mr Redmond was eupported by the Speaker' perhaps the most attracting feature if thin weeks debates was the splendid sense of Slr. Churchill's rejoinder to Lord Llugh Cecil's threat of physical force in the event of the 'tome Rule Bill passing. At this moment mid Mr. Churchill there were some 70,000 meson strike, many of them hungry, most of them poor, holding opinions quite as definitely and as strongly as the" Die Herds "and if these poor men attempted to n-e any force the noble Lord would be the first tome., that the troops should be sent down for stern repression. Whether the Home Rule bogey was an attempt on the Fort of Mr. Balfour to reunite Ids .livid«l ranks we cannot say, but, failing aeon in such an affect it cannot help but advance the flume Rule cause iu the public opinion of this country. Becaues of thie, and because of the incontrovertible fact that the days, nay the hours of oligarchic tyranny—• tyranny ageiest which, O'Connell, Iravi,t, l'aroell and many a brave one of our race died fighting—are ended we repeat that the Irish of to-day have cause to Ift up their hearts and be glad that they are living in such a moving and an inspiring epoch. IRELAND'S INDPSTRIAL PRoGRESS.

If there be one man in Ireland who is better qualified to speak on the industrial and agricultural aspects of Irish life. it is Mr. T. I'. Gill, the Secretary of the Department of Agriculture and Techncial Instruction in Ireland. 11 may be interesting to recall in passing that it was for the unpardonable crime f selecting Mr. ill for this p,aw by reason of 1 his unrivalled expert knowledge that Sir Howe flunkett was bounded out of public life by his Irish Unionikt f lends because Sir llorue had dared to appoint a Catholic Irashman, the creed and the nationality being the sde grounds of Unioniq dissatisfaction. • lb. other day Mr. Gill in addressing a Belfa-t audience exprreeed his opinion that owing to the amazing advance which Ireland's industlies had made in tits last ten years, wit'lin he ann liaet , Ireland was amongst the flout rat idy and roundly progressive nations of the scald. After citing the increase in Ireland's annual turnover in trade, an increase of 2,1 millions in four years, the increasing exports, the upward grade in iimports showing a greater spending power on the part of the people, the ircrease in Irish joint Stec Bank Balances f rota 47i millions seven years aga to 7al millons to-day, Mr. Gill described the progress wl.ich had been made in Education. This is the most encouraging factor of all. Our education is beginning ti look to our agricultural and industrial development. But it is not, we are glad to know, go.ng to do so in any narrow or merely technical sense ; or in any sense which would dirnini,h the reverence rightly due to the human and ethical part of culture. Under the itithrence of men like Mr. GPI, and Mr. O. Russell, the Irish Maeterlinck, whose myatc prose sod 6ne music of verse have caught the trained ears of Enrope aml An:erica, whilst Ids lucid 871,1 practical urn ings on agricultural organisation are valued by every farmer in Ireland, tinder the guidance of men such as these the education of the future will have a broader and a higher aim - the training of good men and good citizens not less than echolars aG,i expert worke:

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, AUGUST 12 mi.

The battle is over, and VETO VICTORY. the victory won! On Thursday night at eleven o'clock the Parliament Bill passed the House of Lords by 131 votes to 114—a Government majority of 17. Lord LANS- DOWNE prevailed over the " die-bards," and prevented the—to him—ignominy of a creation of peers by Royal preragative; and the will of the people prevailed after a struggle which has continued with lesser or greater intensity and bittterness for 80 years. The oldest of those among us who have been accustomed to give some portion of their attention to politics must have the most keen feeling as to th greatness of the thing that has been done. For it is something that is done once and for all,and something that will change in most material respects the political future of our country. It is not a revolution that has taken place; but it is something that if it ha i not been done now would inevitably have produced an actual revolu- tion. The thing is simple enough. The power of the House of Lords permanently to obstruct the will of the people is taken away. A. great power of obstruction and delay will still be left to the peers; but the the House of Lords will not in the future, as in the past, be permitted to delay necessary reforms from one generation to another. The debate which began on Wednesday, and ended in a triumph for the Constitution and the Government on Thursday night marks with a white stone the commencement of a new era.

In the whole history of English politics there has been nothing so remarkable as the differences of opinion which were made manifest by the debate in the House of Lords. Consider the situation. A complete deadlock between the two Houses of Parlia- ment is provided for by the Constitution. It is presumed that Parliament reflects and carries into law the will of the people; but as there are two Houses of Parliament, one of which is in permanent opposition to one Of the great parties in the State, it follows that the will of the people can never be carried into effect with regard to the very greatest measures when the party in power is that party which cannot command the assent of the House of Lords. If there is no remedy for such a state of affairs there would be perma- nent deadlock when the Liberal party was in power. But our far-sighted Constitution has foreseen this difficulty. There is a remedy for the obstruction of the House of Lords. It lies in the creation of more lords in any number that may be required for the pass- ing of a measure which the people have determined upon and the Lords have deter- mined not to pass. This is precisely the situation that arose and was dealt with by a firm hand. As the peers at present in possession did not wish to be swamped by new creations to a number which would cl7eapen their dignity, most of them proved ready, under the pretence that they bad been deprived of their freedom of action, to abstain from voting against a measure which Lwould have been carried against them by the Royal prerogative, whatever course they might choose to adopt. The bulk of the peers prepared themselves to accept the inevitable. Some others to the extent, say, of one-sixth or one-seventh or the members of the House of Lords, deter- mi; ed to do something heroic. They would I vote against the Bill which deprived them of their absolute veto on legislation, knowing very well that nothing could happen which would not take place irrespective of the fact that they had manned the last ditch. Their idea of greatness was based on their lack of humour. They had no notion whatever that they were heroes pour rire. Lord RIBBLESDALL put the case very powerfully and concisely the other day. He is not an enthusiastic sup- porter of the Government; he disagreed with

iber - man

the Government to such an extent, in that some time ago he resigned his mena ehip of ha. But Lord RIBBLESDALE is a with 1 great deal of commonsense; told the House of Lords that their up. In Mr. Balfour's speech MR. BALFOUR'S on Monday we had MISTAKE. again a mistake which

.. REFORM PICNIC. Greena " sang the children as they returned home from the much-advertised picnic to Frodsham on Thursday. It was hardly surprising that feelings of gratitude to her ladyship and her pet organization, the Women's Tariff Reform League, should be uppermost in the juvenile mind, for free trips on a large scale do not happen every day, and where is the healthy boy or girl who would not seize the opportunity of such entertainment, regardless of the source from which it emanated? We are fully conscious of the unpleasantness of the task of criticis- ing the action of any organization which would bring joy into the lives of poor children, but when it is sought to , gain a party advantage under the guise of disinterested charity, it becomes necessary to enter a protest. If the picnic had been the outcome of private benevolence, dictated solely by a desire to give pleasure to the little ones, we should have been the last to find fault with it. Had Lady Greenall pro- posed to give the treat as an evidence of her goodwill and as an acknowledgment of the fact that the family with which she has allied herself has derived so much of its wealth through the industry and thirst of the people of Warrington, we should have welcomed the kindly act. But the picnic ' was promoted by a distinctly political body called the Women's Tariff Reform League. In no sense can the League claim to be a charitable organisation, its sole object being to further tariff reform, and the picnic was obviously designed with that aim in view. Had an election been imminent, the treat would in all probability have been ruled by the law courts to be a corrupt practice. Was it any less corrupt because it was intended to advance tariff reform, to make it popular with the children and thus influence the votes of their fathers when an election comes? We wonder if these Tariff Reformers realized the difficulty in which their project was calculated to place every right-thinking man in the borough? The Tariff Reformer would doubt- less be inclined to say "I want to retain my self-respect and . vote for Tariff Reform . _ .

we have corrected so often (says the " Westminster Gazette, ") that we despair of making the point clear if we have not already (lone so. Mr. Balfour spoke of the King being placed in a position in which . .• . . .

Here we have the complete confusion of votes and electors. Let us repeat that while in England the polling at the last General Election showed a Unionist majority of something like 25,000 in votes, when a due allowance is made for plural votes the actual Liberal majority of voters could not have been less than 125,000. Unionists may make what capital they can out of the small majority they obtained in England in votes and seats, but they must not claim a majority of voters. _

Making a proportionate allowance for the uncontested seats—and deducting, so far as is possible, the plural voters—the Govern- ment had a majority at the General Election of 550,000 votes. That is a huge majority as our elections go in this country. As a fact it gives the Government a much less majority in seats than it might reasonably have anticipated on the basis of the General Elections of 1995 and 1900, which returned large Unionist majorities. Mr. Balfour is guilty of a special variation of the usual con- fusion when he goes on to extend the argu- ment from England to " the people of this country." Here is separation, indeed, for Mr. Balfour would not only exclude Ireland from any voice in our politics, but Wales and Scotland too. As a matter of fact, with- out any allowance for plural voters, in Great Britain the votes cast at the General Election were: Liberal and Labour 2,919,770 Unionist

I The Liberal Free Trader was placed in the dilemma of not wishing to disappoint his children by debarring them from partaking of street along with their school fellows,while at the same time he would properly resent being placed under an obligation—although veiled under the name of charity—to the Women's Tariff Reform League and its ?resident. Stripped of the kindly ideas which were brought forward to cloak the motive, the picnic was nothing more or less than a gigantic bribe to the electors, through their children, to vote for Tariff Reform at the next election. Like all such practices we believe it will have exactly the opposite effect on the independent voters of Warring- ton, who have already been freely expressing their disgust at the introduction of this most objectiorable element into political warfare.

The debate upon Mr. MINISTERS AND Balfour's Vote of Cen- THE CROWN. sure, was .especially

notable for the state- ment in which the Prime Minister, with the authority of and in accordance with the desire of the King, withdrew the veil that a well.founded etiquette requires to be main- tained under normal circumstances about the ' communications passing between the Sovereign and his Ministers. Mr. Asquith pointed out that on April 4th, 1910, he I informed the House of Commons, in language previously communicated to King Edward, that, in the event, which was then understood to bo certain, of the Lords' rejecting the Veto resolutions, he would' immediately " tender advice to the Crown, "and in no case recommend a dis- solution except in circumstances in which, if successful in the election, the will of the people might be carried into effect." But the death of King Edward led to a conference whereby it was sought to settle the constitu- tional question by agreement, and so spare the new Sovereign from the necessity of a grave decision. When the Conference broke down the Government decided to advise a dissolu- tion, but it would obviously have been unjustifiable to invite the electors to pro- nounce again upon the constitutional question without taking steps to ensure that that pronouncement could be carried into effect. The Government therefore laid before his Majesty their view that, if the election yielded an adequate majority for the Parlia- ment Bill, they could not take office unless they had an understanding that his Majesty would be ready, should the Bill be rejected or mutilated by the Lords, to exercise his con- stitutional powers" to secure that effect should be given to the decision of the country." His Majesty signified his assent after consultation with the Prime Minister and Lord Crewe. The elections were held; an adequate, an i more than adequate, majority for the Parliament Bill was obtained, the Lords carried, and evidently intend to insist upon, wrecking amendments to the Bill : and the King signified his will- ingness to create, if necessary, as many new Peers as would be sufficient to carry the Bill, unrnutilated. So the case stood when Mr. Balfour moved his Vote of Censure.

The Board of Trade OUR TRADE. returns for July do not reach the abnormally high level which we have become accustomed to expect in these days. During the month: Imports rose by £1,690,000 to £51,064,000. Exports fell by £3,780,000 to £34,608,000. . . _

Re-exports fell by £42,000 to .£8,178,000. But for the seven months just ended importsl and exports have reached the gigantic total of £643,472,394, or nearly 17 millions more than last year—the " boom " year—during the same period, and 79 millions above 1909. There is every reason to believe that the present set-back in exports is only temporary. 1 he movements of trade this year have been subject to unusual fluctuations arising from various causes, and probably the July figures are only a further example of this. July was characterised by considerable labour disturb- ance at the ports. At Hull, Southampton, Liverpool, Manchester, Glasgow, and in south Wales we had all work of shipping stopped for days together. These disturbances have affected the movements of merchandise, and when the totals for the year come to be made up there is little doubt that the temporary fluctuations will have adjusted themselves, and that the trade of the present year will prove to have been the largest in volume in our history.

The course which the

SHAMELESS Government pursued in QUESTION-BEGGING, the constitutional crisis

was, as Mr. Asquith justly claimed, "correct," "considerate," and "constitutional." In trying to prove the contrary, Mr. Balfour was unusually infelicitous. His reference to King George's " inexperience" in particular struck a jarring note. The fundamental weakness of his position, when he taxed the Government with want of consideration for - the Sovereign, was glaring since the responsibility for forcing a grave decision upon the King necessarily rested with the Leader of the Opposition, who, while he was unable to form an alternative Government which would command a majority in the Commons,persisted in driving matters to the last extremity. But Mr. Balfour endeavoured to escape from this awkward conclusion by the pretext that the King had been deceived. Was the King informed, he asked, that the difference upon which the two Houses might split was solely whether the Bill should pass with all its provisions, "with the exception that Home Rule and some other points, if dealt with by legislation and rejected by the House of Lords," should be "referred" to the con- stituencies? A more shameless example of question-begging phraseology was never pre- sented to the House. The Lansdowne amendments proposed to set up a novel and unconstitutional machinery which would make the Lords entire masters of the political situation, able to reject and " refer ' any measure, even if it involved finance, that They might claim to be of exceptional import- ance. On the other hand, the constituencies at the last General Election distinctly refused to endorse the Ilalfonr-Lansdowne Referendum proposals, and as distinctly Approved the inclusion in the provisions of the Parliament Bill of "Home Rule and all ether points."

Among the Government supporters of the Parliament Bill in the House of Lords division were 37 Unionist Peers and 13 prelates. These gentlemen are described by the "Times" as ' patriotic " and by the "die-hards" as " traitors " and men who put coronets before conscience. The split in the Tory ranks does not seem likely to heal yet awhile. o _ •

The only change in the outlook with regard to Warrington Bridge is the fact that the contract with Messrs. Alfred Thorne and Sons, the bridge builders, has now been signed. This should augur well for an early start being made with the work, and it is indeed high time that the justifiable impatience of the public was appeased by some move in the matter. * *

One feature of the tariff Reform picnic to Frodsham was the labelling of all the children with Tariff Reform badges. No doubt this was necessary to prevent any far-sighted Frodsham youngsters from mingling with the crush and getting a free treat, but the labels provoked some amuse- ment among Free Trade papas and mamas when the young hopefuls returned.

The Wom3n's Tariff Reform League picnic has been such a pronounced success that the Liberals ought to cast about to go one better. Could they not arrange to take all the War- rington Parliamentary electors (between the ages of 21 and 90) to Winwick, or some other accommodating country resort? Under the present laws there seems to be no limit to what may done in the name of charity, so why not bribe the people who really have the votes!

We are informed (though we did not see them/ that the collecting boxes for the great picnic which adorned the bars of the tied- houses bore the words "Treat to Slum Children." No one imagines that there are seven or eight thousand slum children in Warrington, and the merry crowd of youngsters who wended their way to the station on Thursday were on the whole remarkably well-dressed. The unfortunate thing is that when they had gone the slums seemed as full of ragged urchins as ever. Those who had needed the picnic most were left behind.

An intimate friend of Edison's, seeing a light burning after midnight in his laboratory one night in 1893, went in and found him so absorbed in his experiments that he seemed almost unconscious of the world around him " Tom," said he, " it's after twelve. Aren't you going home to-night?" " Twelve twelve," replied the g!-eat inventor, waking up from his review. "Twelve ! by George! Now I come to think of it, I was married I to-day. I must go home, sure!" Such is ' the story told in his biographies of the wedding-day of the remarkable genius who is now visiting London.

" Observer " write; to tell us that the other day as he was leaving the park a group of four Councillors were leaving the Town Hall—possibly, he continues, the Town Hall Parks and Baths Committee. This body, I take it, formulated the Rules and Regula- tions for the conduct of the park and doubtless "Keep to the walk " was one of these rules. The nearest way to the Conservative Club is across the green lawn, and our worthy representatives took thie " path "and kept to it. Numerous nippers— too young for the Frodsham outing—watched them. Doubtless some day they will be "boxed " by the gardeners for following the example of these thoughtful legislators.

Arrangements are being made for the celebration of the centenary of John Bright's birth at Rochdale in November. Nothing Pefinite has been agreed upon as to the form the celebration is to take, but it has been decided to invite Lord Rosebery to deliver the chief speech in connection with the event.

, - o • "Ringing the changes " is perhaps the simplest as well as the most puzzling of swindles. A typical ar.d simple case occurred thus : A labourer asked a tobacconist for a pennyworth of tobacco. This is a recent police court case. He gave her a sixpence, Ind she gave him sd. change. He put a penny to the five-pence and asked for 6d. She gave him the sixpence, and he put it with the coppers, and remembered he wanted a shilling in silver ; so he gave the sixpence and the coppers for a shilling—and went away satisfied; ;

If the tariff is the mother of the trusts, corruption is the child of special privilege. This is a historical truth, though the status of the corruption has gradually shifted with the decay of personal and increase of popular government. , . . The whole tariff deal , is a dicker (a deal) in the artificial prices of domestic monopoly, by which the common people think they are gatting an advantage in prices and wages at the expense of, foreigners, while the enjoyers of special privilege know that they are getting an unjust benefit at the expense of all the people. This continual debasement of moral standards cannot go on without corrupting all the people in their business and political relations. Business and political graft are the fruit of education in the school of special privilege.—" Minneapolis Morning Tribune" (U.S.A.), January 27th.

Dr. Andrew Carnegie told an audience at Liverpool the other day that he had" taken to library forming because his father did it before him." William Carnegie's efforts at library founding were, an Edinburgh contemporary points out, extremely modest as compared with the work of his distinguished son. In 1808 Mr. Carnegie's Ifather was a journeyman weaver in Dunfermline. He and another two drivers of the shuttle met, and agreed to convey the lbooks they had in their respective homes to one house. The combined libraries only figured out twenty volumes. The pioneers were joined by other weavers, and they agreed to tax themselves to the extent of al few pence per month and from this fund purchase new books. The little institution ultimately blossomed into the Dunfermlior, Tradesmen's Library. •

I Under the headin; " k karernong ' fate," "The Arbitrator" for this month (which is edited by Mr. Fred Maddison) says " It is really pathetic to see how Mr. Robert Blatchford hugs the delusion that he has saved the Empire from some awful catastrophe. He imagines that it was his articles in the Jingo Press which !orced the Government to increase the Navy Estimates and so defer to a later date that German invasion which gives him so much trouble. The idea that Mr. Blatchford was as a voice crying in the wild?rness in favour of more warships is too funny for words. Of course, the truth is that he was just one of a crowd of alarmists, and his special value was not what he wrote, but his name. For years he had been the most successful writer in the Socialist ranks, and to enlist him in the service of the scare- mongers was regarded as a great gain. It is true that he did ,little more than sell a few more copies of a Tory paper, but it was an experiment which might have succeeded. All he did was to have his name coupled with Lord Charles Beresford as the champion humorists of two elections. A political leader has rarely had a worse fall."

The Lords and their friends (says the "Manchester Guardian ") have come to grief because they have refused to recognise the primary law of English constitutional exist- ence—that each of the parts of the Constitu- tion lives subject to a fixed constitutional check upon disloyalty to the others. If an English King is undutiful, the Commons can refuse him Supply—that is the check on the Crown. If an English House of Commons is ' undutiful, the Sovereign can dissolve it—that is the check on the Commons. And if an English House of Lords is undutiful, then, as Professor Dicey says—: But here the peers shout No. That there should be any check on them is an "outrage," a "crime," "felony," "treason," Unlike the Crown, they are free from the check of Supply. Unlike the Commons, they are free from the check of D:ssolution. And now, with abuse, threats, and even some oaths, they demand to be free from their own allotted check too —the Crown's right to '• swamp" them when it agrees with its Ministers that they have persistently set up their will against that of the nation. It has been on their side a revolutionary enterprise, an attempt at I usurpation and at the defeat of the intention and spirit of the Constitution by releasing , one of its parts from all constitutional I restraint. Sober-minded Englishmen generally ' will be glad that it has failed, and almost every day now brings them additional reasons for being glad that it has failed.

There is the retention of our naval supremacy and the urgent necessity of putting in order our plans for Imperial

befenc.

In the course of his kingly duties the I reigning monarch is called on to speak on Art, on Literature, on Science, on Hygiene I and on a host of other questions. Shall the King be denied the right to speak out on Imperial affairs which do not hinge on party politics? Surely not. There are many matters upon which the Empire requires guidance. And with the memory of that great 'Wake up, England ' speech still fresh upon us, surely we may look to guidance, direct, plain, commonsense guidance, from King George. On all great Imperial matters men of every shade of political creed would listen to him with attention and respect. WARRINGTON WESLEYAN CYCLING CLUB

For once during the season on Saturday last the club were prevented from their run to Penner by a rain yday, but the abisence of any grumbling marked ths apprecia- tion of the cool and refreshing downfall. CHRIST CHURCH, P AUG ATE.

Christ Church, Padgate, is a comparatively modern building, the parish hav'n t been formed in 1838 as an off-shoot of the ancient parish of Warrington. The church, which was built when the Rev. and Hon. Horace Powys was Rector of Warrington, has a pleasant rural situation and its interior arrangements are tasteful and dignified. There are two beautiful large stained glass windows and a handsome reredos. The present vicar of Padgate is the Rev. G. W. Dickinson, M.A., a former curate at the \Warrington Parish Church.

NEW WE iLEIAN MINiFER FOR PA')GATE

The Rev. Ebenezer Morgan, of Liverpool, has been appointed to succeed the Dv. Thomas Wadding at Padgate Wes'eyan Church. Mr. Morgan was born at Abe .ystwyth ia /452 and, like many another Wedeln began his cateer in training for a sch tat,. It was while be was an aradatant master at Dewabt ry that he offered himself as a candidate for the ministry and , ' as accepted. After leaviog coll.ge, Mr. Morgan's first circuit was Hail filt rate-yard) where he remained two years, afterwards going to Liverpool (il rove- street). Since then lie has travelled in some of the most important circuits in Methor ism and dining his super- intendence of the Birmingham (Ant a Park) circuit he had the ea isfaztion of seeing the long-standing debts oa va-i Ali trusts reduced t, the extent of nearly I. In the course a an appr-ciation in " 'The Methodist Tmies " Mr. Harry Lindsay writes, • Rut ' Mr Morgan is not merely a financial genius ; he has • . time for other things also. A warmer temperance advc,ste no circuit could wish. I remember hearing on, of Mr. Morgan's temperance addretves in hie Cardiff days, when he was temperance secretary in the Cardiff and Sera nsea District, and the impression ,cieated by his fery our and f. .reef alness can never pass away from my mind. Many of tl e readers of ' The Methodist Times ' sill recall some of his con- tribution, to the Press on temperance subjects.

Of c:tirse, Mr. Morgan Las nublisted many other things, and whi'e on this point I might mention his remarkable little pamphlet, ' Why I run a Methodist,' of which the late Rev. Thomas Champne,s printed and sold 19,Trt copies for the benefit of the " Joyful .!:erwe Mission. Besides these, Mr. If irgau 11 the author of two volumes of sermons, ' Christ ut the Well' and ' The Calls of God,' b .th priblisbesi by ' our own Book-room. By the bye. Professor Stalker, the author of ' Imago Christ, has spoken very favourably of ' The Calls of God.' Writing to Mr. Morgan. the Professor says:—' I la,ve long been acquainted wirh your The Calls of God.' . . I often read your book with ap•recia- tion, and I know it to be a solid and vital piece of nork which well deserves not only the success it has already attained but a still wider diffusion." Throughout his ministry Mr. Morgan has always kept abreast of the times. Not only is he on rap:ore with the latest theological scholarship but in the wider fields of human affairs he is always to ths forefront. For instance, with regard to the new movements for de equipping and training of boys, Mr. Morgan is an ardent disciple and an enthusiastic worker. Be believes that It ye Brigades and Bops' Life Brigades are capable of rendering efficient work, not only to the lads themselves, but to the Church and to the country at large. The a object of the Boys' Life Brigades is to lead the boys into the service of Christ and to train them for an actve. di.ciplined and useful manhood, and hence Mr. Morgan throws himself heart and soul into the work. Tones him in his regimentals, as chaplain of a brigade, is a sight for sore eyes, and ..hows how well a Methodist minister can adapt himself to the changing times. Once you put a bell and a cap on a boy," he says, " the rid...dater acquires an influence over that boy which he might not otherwise obtain." Answer- ing the question whether such training does n.t savour of orilitarism, he insists that the object of the Brigade, is not to make the boys into soldiers but to teach them the habits of self-respect, obedience, courtesy, and he!pfulness to . other.. n . With such up-to-dste ess hi• methods, it is little wonder that Mr. Morgan is the successful minister that he is. Somehow, he seems to have found the secret of perjetual youth. It is true that the " Minutes " would give the lie to that statement, but even the " Minutes " must not always I* regarded as an infallible witness in such matters A man is never any older than he feels, and Mr. Morgan's wholeheartedness in all be undertake., his L ready accerauce of ar y new tnethols that he feels

will make for the bluer ekd of the Church or for the good of the people themselves, his lively interest in all human affairs, and above all his cheery optimism, prove him to he a very young man, despite the calendar. FIELD FIRING WITH BALL CARTIIIDUE.

This morning reveille was sounded at 3.45. Coffee was served at 4 15 and at 5 o'clock the battalion moved off to take part in a new experience. All the brig.de, together with the Artillery, were ordered to attack Farlet to Fell from the east of Lupton. Instead of the usual blank ammunition, the 10th (Scottish) Liverpools were armed with ball cartridges. Great care had been taken to keep the spec'ators fr.ma the danger area. Targets had !teen erec'ed on the IdII to represent the enemy and on the conclusion of firing, these were inspected and favourably reported span. The rest t f the brigade and the artillery kept up an incessant fire with blank ammunition on the flanka ' and in the rear. 0.1 the 'charge" being sounded, a a magnificent spectacle was witnessed, the Scottish " doubling " up the hill in splendid f,rmation, covered by a pouring fire from the rest of the troops. A Inum'oer of men were ordered to fall out as casualties. Breakfast was served at and the battalion moved off at 0,30 to take part in a tactical scheme. This parade was held in shirt sleeves, • change mach appreciated by the men as there cans broiling sun. The' enemy" con- sisted of a skeletoa force wearing whits hat bands, under the commend of Mejor Appleton and Sergeant- Instructor Hackett. A galling fire was poured in from the front and on both flanks, and when the time came for the final charge, the men rushed up the 101 l with shouts of victory. The weather is very hot and many men left camp In the afternom. Tourtantv.

FLYING KITES IN JAPAN

In Japan there is an annual feast day for boys, when each house having male children hangs out strings of paper carp, which inflated by the breeze, becomes lifelike monster fish. "It was on this feast day," says a writer in the "Wide World," "that we left Yokohama for Kamakura, once the eastern cmital of Japan, now merely a quiet little seaside village. "As it was such an important occasion, the whole world made holiday, some families hurried to the seashore to fly their enormous humming kites, from which the parents appeared to derive quite as much enjoyment as the children. The loud hum emitted by the soaring kite is caused by a piece of thin bamboo, which is stretched tightly across from shoulder to shoulder.

" This taut bamboo filament not only acts as an wolian harp but bends the whole kite, so that its surface is concave, instead of being, as in our kites, a plane. The noise when some three-score or so of these monsters are in the air at the same time is deafening. "The Japanese kite has no tail, but is furnished with numerous long streamers. Great competitions are held by the owners of the kites, and occasionally a mimic battle will be fought in the air, the rival factions endeavouring by means of powdered glass, which has been previously worked into a definite length of the kite strings, to saw through a rival's string, and so bring the vanquished kite tumbling ignominiously to the ground."

===== 0002601_19110812_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW CHILDREN ARE POISONED.

Every summer and autumn we read o 1 children who have been poisoned, as a result of eating some berries, leaves, or other parts of plants which they have picked up in the woods or hedges. One of the first things boys and girls should learn is never to eat any berries except the well-known harmless ones, such as blackberries, wild strawberries, or raspberries. Little folks must be watched till they are old enough to recognise edible fruits, but even when very young they may be taught not to try eating anything which is unknown.

The plants that aro most commonly responsible for illness and even death when eaten by children are privet berries, laburnum seeds, aconite leaves, the brilliant red, juicy berries of the arum (lords and ladies), and the woody nightshade. The deadly night- shade is pretty well-known to be poisonous, but it is far less common than the woody nightshade or bitter-sweet. The common hemlock is a strong poison, but there is nothing to tempt children to eat it; one danger, however, lies in its hollow stem, from which whistles may be made, and these of course, when put to the mouth will convey the poisonous juice to the membranes and the saliva, by which means it may reach the stomach.

Floating, drifting, Now uplifting, Lost at last in heaven's clear blue! THIS WEEK'S STORY. HER DIFFICULTY.

A school teacher asked her pupils to draw a picture of that which they wished to be when they grew up. The class went dili- gently to work with tablets and pencils, drawing pictures of policemen and sailors and nursemaids and fine ladies—all except one little, girl who sat quietly holding her pencil in hand and looking abstractedly in front of her.

The teacher, noticing her said : " Don't you know what you want to be when you grow up?" " Yes, I know," answered the little woman looking up ; " I know ; I want to be married but I didn't know how to draw it!"

PILGRIMAGES

THE BIRTHPLACE OF TENNYSON.

A DAY AT SOMERSBY

Last Monday the still lanes that lead to Somersby were stirred to unusual life. It is to be doubted whether this quiet Lincoln- , shire village ever witnessed a real crowd, for, it lies hidden in a desolate tract undisturbed by railways, and fully seven miles from the nearest station. The attraction that drew the unwonted multitude was the opening of 'Somersby Church and the unveiling of a bust of Tennyson. The Church has been restored in honour of the poet, whose early I years were spent here, and the bust is placed in the chancel in his memory. The occasion and the speeches suggest to us that many readers may like to know a, little of the village that gave birth to our much-valued Tennyson. Eight miles from Horncastle (the end of a single line of railway), we came upon the tiny, unspoiled hamlet of Somersby, and find an afternoon much too short in which to drink in its splendid interest. Not that there is much in the village to detain us. If we were here without knowledge of Tenny- son, a brief visit would suffice. The interest lies in the fact that it was here the great poet received his first impressions, that these lanes and homes and running streams inspired his earliest poetry, and are reflected in his magical lines. The poet Goethe laid it down that Ile who would roetry's secret know Must to the realm poetic go; Who would the poet underatand Must trarel in the poet's land. This is splendidly true and of no one more so than the poet Tennyson. Lincolnshire scenery is mirrored in all his poetry. What Scott did for the Highlands and Wordsworth for the Lake District,

LOCKSLEY HALL

.... The next step in the pilgrimage Lakes us Ito the neighbouring village of Bag Enderby. Dr. Tennyson held the joint iwings :if the two parishes, and to this quaint, old-world church the Tennywns frequently came to worship. The church is unrestored, and the interior is far more interesting than that at Bomersby. The stone floor, the curiously carved font, at least 500 years old, the worm- eaten pews, the great square seat for the squire, all combine to give a good idea of what an English church was like in the olden time.

Further along the Spilsby-roarl, high up on the left is Langton Hall, the Locksley Hall of the poems: Tie the place, and all around it, u of old the curlews call, Dreary gleams about the moorland flying over Lockslry Dall.

At Spilsby our journey ends. The pilgrim of ampler leisure may see much more, but for ourselves we have no complaint. If only a day, it has been spent in the company of an immortal, and it leaves us full of rich content. AM.

THE_ BROOK

The road ascends from Horncastle to the summit of the wolds and gives fine dews in all directions. Leaving the main road at the dictation of a finger post we follow a winding lane until we reach sight of the" woods that belt the grey bill side' above the hidden village of Somersby. The road dips into a hollow and we get our first Tennysonian thrill, in finding ourselves upon the bridge that spans "The Brook." The stream lives in his "Farewell."

Flow down, cold rivulet, to the sea, Thy tribute wave deliver; No more by thee my steps shall be, For ever and for ever.

And although the poet declared that his "Brook " was purely imaginary no one will be persuaded that the stream which " chatters over stony ways" is any other than this Somemby beck. It lives also in his "Ode to memory."

The brook that loves To yurl o'er matted mum and ribb6rl sand, Or dimple in the dark of rushy coves, Drawing into hie narrow earthern urn, In every elbow and turn, The filmed tribute of the rough moorland.

The road that leads onward to the village nas another great association for hero upon a slimmer morning at five o'clock that Tennyson wrote the haunting lines.

Break, break, break, On by cold grey stones, I/ sea! And I would that my tongue could utter The thoughts that aria° in mo.

It is true that his thoughts were far away upon the crags above the Severn, silt it was here that the magic lines were woven. THE CHURCH AND RECTORY.

To our left as we reach the village is the church, "restored' out of much of its ancient beauty, but happy in the possession of a fine fourteenth century churchyard cross. We see too, the grave of Dr. Tennyson and the font from which the poet was baptised. The Rectory, Tennyson's birth place, holds us much longer than the Church. It is an ideal poet's house, standing between a thatchroofed house and a castellated, strange looking dwelling, screened by tall chestnut trees from the road. In the front is a green velvet lawn, with flowers and engirdling shrubs. The holly hedge planted by the old doctor still flourishes, but of

The poplars foss Which Magid breads my fsther's door, there is no trace. The house is picturesque and its aspect is semi-ecclesiastical. The roofs are tiled and steep, and the dining-room, with its long-pointed stained glass windows leads one to believe that it was originally intended for a private chapel. Its rambling appearance is owing to the old rectory having been made up of two houses; for situation the place could not be excelled. The beet aspect of the house is from the

rear and this to the literary pilgrim is the hallowed spot. The lawn we have often seen in imagination and now we see it in reality. At the rear of the church, some distance along the Tetford road, is Holywell Glen, a favourite haunt of the poet, and referred to in" Maud" When I was woot to meet her In the silent woody places,— By the home that tie, me birth, We stod tranced rn long embraces Mixt with kisses sweeter, sweeter Than anything on earth.

It was in this glen that Tennyson carved the words " Byron is dead," and spoke of that dire event as though a light of the world had bsen quenched.

"Oh! I thought elm had taken it; and if she had I ahould have gone mad!" thought • the poor girl. 'dud night Luey'n father slept at it, Pad dock Row, and both Cook and Lucy were well pleased with the arrangement. She had felt a good deal of lassitude and some headache on the day that it occurred; but these 'lmp- tome had all passed. and she wan well and as strong as usual. She had a great deal of business to attend to, and each day a deeper sense of satisfaction filled her as she remem- bered that Gerard Lonsdate was coming to claim the diamond. "When he has it, I shall be free. I never knew that anything no valuable could be such a curse," thought the girl. "That black ribbon might be weighted with lead, that diamond might be a stone dragging me to earth, no weary am I of carrying it on my person. All day it haunts me, and all night I live through fearful dangers in my dreams."

DIZO NING AND LIFE-SAVING

In a quotation reproduced in these columns a week ago, it was questioned whether very great pro- green bad been made in the latter in relation to the former. As to the avoidance a the " drnth-clutch," one experienced swimmer t Id sin a while ago that the rescuer an seized should press back the thumbs of the panic-stricken drowning one. I readmo, that the old tradition that a drowning pewitu rises three times before he go's down, while it sounds well in Carnegie medal stales is not true. A Kraut rises 11,1 many tile' a as he can get to the surface—which may be once or • hundred times—and he drowns when he is so full of twat r that lie cannot breathe. As to attempts at renunciation, respecting which ignorance is likewise said to abound, we are authoritatively told that the first thinga to Ito done are to open out the clothing so as to exp we the body down to the waist, clear olintructione from the mouth, nom, or throat, draw out the tongue and keep ;t out by tying IS piece of tape round it. Then ernpty Vie water out of the lungs by turning the patient face downward., putting a firm roll of clothing under the chest and stomach, and pressing two or three time., for four or five ileClll,lB each time, upon the patient's back. Now turn the patient over and give him two or three smart slap. on the stomach or the cheat with the open hand. Place a cushion (a coat rolled up will serve) finder the shoulder blades, allowing the head to fall back. Stand at the patient's head, grasp his arms just above the elbows, draw them gently and steadily upwards above the bead, and keep them stretched upwards for two seconds. Turn down the patient's arms and press dam gently sod firmly for two seconds against the sides of the chest, crowing the forearms over the pit of the stomach. Repeat these MOVOIM•11,15 alternatively, deliberately and perseveringly about fifteen times a minute, until you see signs of natural breathing. Do not leave off your efforts under two hours, fir cases I have been known where resuscitati .n has occurred in that time. After breathing has been induced, but not before, apply friction by rubbing the limbs upward., and apply hot flannels or hot bottles to the pit of the stomach, arm-pits, and soles of the feet. Then, lastly, give wine, warm brandy and water, or coffee. I reprint these directions, not by any :towns, because in three days of enthusiasm in regard to artilmlance work, it is poseible to bdieve that they are generally needed, but on account of the expression of opinion quoted from the "Country (Modem/to," a week ago. Lie tr whom I am indebted for the foregoing 1 adds for the benefit of the uninitiated—

7-lion't 'h.; to fr:a-rn the role, off br heart. Jest reed them aml folloq the operations in y.mr mind while you are doing eo. If you do this and then try and follow the procedure from meinory once or twice you. will hare a sufficient grup of them to bring them to your mind when they are required. " There are many civilian@ as well as member. of the borough police force who could bear this out, fur that is how they have themselves attained efficiency in such beneficent work.

NO NEW PEERS!

LORDS PASS THE VETO BILL.

The Die-Herds Last Effort.

The final scene of the great Veto drama was played in a crowded House of Lords on Thursday evening. It was a spectacle strange enough. Arrayed against each other were Lord Halsbury's obstinate "No Surrender' party and the little band of Liberals, the bulk of the Conservative party abstaining with Lord Lansdowne from voting. Various small bands watched each other jealously ; if one showed symptoms of voting the other were determined to go into the opposite lobby. Would the Halsburyites, after all, win and force the creation of Peers? That was the question everyone was asking on this fateful night. So doubtful seemed the issue that Lord Morley's weighty statement early in the proceedings bore a special significance, particularly in view of the Halsburyites' assumption that the Government were " bluffing." Said Lord Morley : If the Bill Mould be defeated to-night, his Majesty w. old assent ti a creation of peers sufficient in numbers to guard against any possible combination of the different parties in opposition, by which the Parliament Bill !night be txpowd a second Moe to defeat. Every vote given L-night against my motion not to insist on what is called the lanedowne amendment is a rote in favour of a liege and i prompt creation of piers , The" Die-Hards" were smitten by other speakers, such as the Duke of Devonshire— who said significantly that if the Unionist leaders' advice was rejected their position would become " intolerable and impossible" and the Archbishop of Canterbury, who said he would vote for the Government because of the " callousness" of the extremists.

The crucial division took place late at night when there voted :

Extraordinary Travelling.

Sir,—On Bank Holiday a party of about 50 succumbed to the wiles of the London and North- Western Railway Company, and look advantage of the excursion to Windermere. The return journey commenced at 9 p m., and we should have reached Warrington (according to the promise of the Railway Company) at 11.25, but it was almost 3 o'clock before we arrived. The journey from Windermere to Preston was done in fair time, the latter town being reached shortly after 11) o'clock. The extraordinary rate of travelling was from Preston to Warrington, 27 miles in something over four hours, ie. about six miles an hour. Of course, all fairminded persons are prepared to make some allowance for the rash of Bank Holiday traffic, but surely sun have a right to expect that the Railway Company should only arrange for such traffic as they can cater for with safety and with s.mething like reasonable despatch.— Yours, &c., CHAUVED.

Sir,--The onen air debate between Messrs. II Henshall and If. New cm claim to be the most successful ever held in Warrington. This was evidenced by the fact that according to estimate. fully s,teto individuals listened to it a ithout undue interruptions. The subject of controversy to attract so large a gathering - under such conditions cannot therefore be considered to be a " dead one" but • " living issue " of great vitality. There was no loss of temper and no diecourteous remark. As a debate it was quite a shining example in this respect. Owing to the manner in which the debate was con- ducted—the great attraction it proved, the great interest excited—the light given to many who are not blinded by party prejudice and can rise also above party environment and the remarks that have been made by many who were present that they would like the two debaters to meet again under similar auspices lam impelled 0 ask whether the Tariff Reform League and the Free Tiede lrbim can see their way to arrange for a further deba.e between Mews. U. Henshall and H. New.—Yours faithfully, Warrington, August 10th. 1911

The impossibility of Preference with India.

Sir,—! have pointed out in season and perhaps also out of season that Tariff Reform aa advocated in Great Britain and Tar it Reform as advocated in India are two entirely different and incompatible things. The former involves the admission of British goods into India free of duty ; the latter has as its first principle the exclusion of British Roods, especially Lancashire cottons, from India. Obviously to amount of logic-chopping can make these two things the same. Tariff Reformers have been I ath to accept this view, in spite of the native Indian speeches which I have instanced as proving its correctness. Writing to "Tire Times "of last Monelsy,Mr.Wadia, a prominent India cotton manufacturer, nays Mr. Balfour end the Tariff Reformers are willing to use India for their own Impose whibt denyirg to-India that Protection which they find essential for themselves If they are going to give an what Mr. Balfour and his friends intend, I would rather et., India remain fiscally as she is." _

nwsiir as wis is. If Mr. Balfour and the Tariff Reformers use the arguments of Indian Protectionists to support tleir own programme of Imperial Preference, how long will they ho able to resist the demand for the Protec- tion which India orally wants, namely, against Great Britain? What that cit mean will hr best realised from a speech of Mr. Bepin Chandra Pal : " The moment we have the right of self-taxation what tball wedo ? . . . we ohall impose a heavy prohibitive protective tat if! upen every inch of textile fabric from Manchester, upon every blade of knife that comes from &eds. What will the condition of I.aneashire be then ? "In 1909 we sent to It ilia 2,193.000,000 yards of cotton piece-goods. "—Yours faithfully,

W. ENEVER TODD, Statistical Secretary, (Free Trade Union).
