£4l, as compared with the average cost of £23 due to other maladies. Using these figures as a basis for the experience of all societies we arrive at the total outgo of £1,120,000, apart from the amount given at death by some of the societies. THE NATIONAL WASTE.

But this only covers a comparatively small part of the national cost of consumption. The annual expenditure of the Poor Law Guardians for indoor treatment of those patients specially treated as consumptives, is calculated to be £420,000 per annum. The outdoor relief, due to this illness, and the destitution caused thereby, must be greatly in excess of the amount expended on indoor treatment. We have further the amount spent by charitable institutions and private individuals,which it is impossible to calculate except by introducing a great deal of guess work. We can, however, get some idea of the amount expended by considering one Ibranch of charitable work. Taking the charitable institutions for the special treat- ment of consumptives in separate buildings, and using the published balance sheets, including of course proportion of upkeep of Institution, we find the cost on the average is £lOO per bed. We have it on the authority of Dr. Latham and Charles Garland in their admirable book "The Con- quest of Consumption," that there are 4,172 beds, which at £l.OO per bed gives an annual total of £417,200. No doubt such calcula- tions are liable to error, but it is probable they err mostly on the side of under-estimating. Taking, however, costs fairly accurately !known, and adding to them the serious I deduction due to loss of wage-earning power and of life capital, but leaving out the unknown quantity of personal expenditure and private and privately organized charity we arrive at the huge sum of over £9,000,000 spent annually in England and Wales. We have thus an enormous material, national loss, which could be saved if this disease could be stamped out. And all who have any authority to speak bold that it could be stamped out. or at least reduced to the narrow limits in which cholera manifests itself in this country. But this material aspect is perhaps the minor consideration. The larger question is the amount of human suffering and want which this disease entails, the homes that are broken up by the death of the breadwinner, the widows with a weight too heavy to be borne, crushed down and reduced to want, and the children robbed of a parent's care and love THE CAUSES.

Happily the causes which produce con- sumption are now well known and are becoming more and more clearly defined. The medical profession know the bacillus which produces the disease, and the manner in which it enters the body and attacks the lungs and other organs. We now know that it is an infectious disease, and is communi- cated frennently by contaminated milk (the bovine type), and also directly from one point to another, also that it is not hereditary although the conditions which create tendencies may be. We now know that its inception can be prevented, and that it is curable in its early stages. We further know what is perhaps after all of greater importance, that the disease cannot make headway in a thoroughly healthy body, and further that the predisposing causes can be removed. Yr. John Burns has aptly described consumption as "the child of poverty, the daughter of ignorance, the offspring of drink, and the product of carelessness." Professor Brouerdel says "that a public-house is the purveyor of consumption, and that alcohol is the most potent factor in its propagation." The medical protession generally agree that alcohol predisposes the individual to tuberculosis by its paralysing action, and its asphyxiating influence on the cellular pro- toplasm, which when so affected is no longer in a condition to resist the invasion of a parasite. Consumption follows fairly closely the poverty line as well as the line of alcoholic consumption. As pauperism diminishes, consumption declines. Except where had housing and drink counteract the effect of high wages and cheap food, tubercu- losis recedes as the general comfort advances.

===== 0002601_19110812_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR AGAINST CONSUMPTION.

'THE CAPTAIN OF THE MEN OF DEATH."

By C. E. Parker)

There are 40,000 deaths from pulmonary tuberculosis (i.e. consumption of the lungs) in England and Wales each year; if we include Ireland and Scotland the number is nearly doubled. Taking the average dura- tion of life from the inception of the disease to the time of death as five years, there must be at least 200,000 persons in England and Wales suffering from consumption. The proportion in Ireland and Scotland is still greater. More people die from consumption I alone in one year than officers and men killed in the three and a quarter years of the South African war. The number of deaths in Warrington from this disease is about 80 annually, which would indicate that there are constantly about 400 persons in the Borough who are enduring the long drawn ] out suffering peculiar to this complaint, and whose lives are darkened by the ever present ] shadow of impending death. In addition to the deaths from consumption there are about fifty annually from other forms of tubercolosis. The greatest mortality is at the period when life is most valuable, that is between 35 and 45 years of age, an age when a person is in his, or her, prime, and when such have others depending upon them for parentage and support. The disease kills one in every ten of the population of England and Wales. In Ireland it kills one in every six (1906). There are however signs of improvement in Ireland due largely to the efforts of the Women's National Health Association headed by Lady Aberdeen.

===== 0002601_19110812_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TOLL ON THE WORKERS.

While the average-number of deaths from consumption is one in ten, i.e taking deaths of all classes in account, the mortality ' amongst the working class adults taken separately suggests a higher average. The Ireturns of those Friendly Societies that have kept records of this particular disease indicate Ithis. The Ancient Order of Foresters (in 11901) show that amongst their members the 'deaths from consumption of the lungs were 15.11 per cent of the total number of deaths, say one in every seven, and the ratio of these deaths to the total membership was 19 per 'thousand. Amongst the wives and widows of members the loss from consumption was 13 23 per cent and the ratio 15 per thousand. The Hearts of Oak Benefit Society in 1904 shows a loss as high as 18 per cent, over one in every six. There is reason to believe that the poorer class of wage earners who do not as a rule join Friendly Societies show a larger average of deaths from this disease than is indicated by the above figures. Poverty 1 and consumption go together as cause and effect._

T. E. Hayward at the British Con- ference on Tuberculosis gave the result of I some calculations as to the effect of this disease on the expectation of life. He finds That supposing consumption were extermin- ated the average length of life for each individual born would increase 21 years, while the average expectation of life of those who survive to 15 years of age would have an increase by about 3V4 years. High as the death rate is, the amount paid in the relief ot sickness by Friendly Societies is relatively higher, ranging between 20 to 25 per cent, of the whole of the sick relief given. It is very evident that if the sickness due to other forms of tuberculosis were added to that of the lungs, quite a quarter of the total sick money distributed by Friendly Societies would bo due to tuberculosis in one form or other. The amount distributed by Friewily Societies for the relief of sickness is £4,060,00. annually ; therefore, the amount paid to com- sumptive cases will be about £1,000,000. The experience of the Hearts of Oak Benefit Society shows that the average coot of each consumptive member receiving benefit, is

===== 0002601_19110812_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
PROGRESS MADE.

The study of the disease picturesquely called by John Bunyan "The Captain of the Men of Death,' ' the means employed and the knowledge gained to battle with this "Death Captain "are already bearing fruit. During the last forty years the deaths from tuberculosis in England and Wales have been

reduced over fifty per cent; in Scotland over- forty per cent., while in Ireland there has been a slight increase during the whole period but signs of improvement are evident in the- last few years. Of European con ntries England leads the way. Of the capi tale or these countries London leads. But this is no reason for relaxing our efforts. The scourge in England is still great, the death roll still high, the human suffering still awful. Everyone worthy of the name of Englishman should take his part in this department of National Service and by private effort, or by aiding collective action, should assist in ridding these islands of this terrible scourge.
